INTRODUCTIOxX. 

The  sources  of  the  services  and  ordinances  of  the  Use  of  the 
Order  of  S.  Gilbert  of  Sempringham  are  four  in  number.  So 
far  as  I  know  there  are  no  other  Gilbert ine  books  now  extant. 
The  four  are  these  : 

1.  A   Missal,  forming  in  its  present  state  a  Missale  parvum 

more  or  less.     Line.  MS.  115. 

2.  The   Kalendar  of  the  Gilbertine  Use  and  those  Mass 

services  which  were  peculiar  to  the  Gilbertine  Use. 
S.  John's  Coll.  Camb.  MS.  239. 

3.  The  Hour  Office  of  S.  Gilbert,  and  Mass.     Bodl.  Digb. 

36,  fo.  1106. 

4.  The  Gilbertine  Ordinale.     Pemb.  Coll.  Camb.  MS.  226. 

I.  This  is  a  composite  book,  or  perhaps  would  be  better 
described  as  a  Gilbertine  Missal  showing  the  process  of  the 
development  of  the  Gilbertine  Mass  Use.  It  consists  of  three 
different  sections  : 

(«)  The  original  book,  now  the  central  portion  of  the 
volume.  This  is  a  Collectar,i  containing  the  Mass 
prayers  of  the  Tempo  rale,  Sanctorale  and  Commune. 
At  the  beginning  is  written  the  "  Ambrosian  " 
prayer  in  a  different  but  contemporary  hand  (the 
first  part  of  which  has  been  removed  and  rewritten 
by  a  later  hand  at  the  time  of  the  subsequent 
adding  of  the  "  Supplement  "  to  the  beginning  of 
the  volume),  and  in  the  original  hand  an  Index  of 
Saints  for  whom  the  Common  is  to  be  used.  To 
this  index  other  names  have  been  added  by  another 
hand.  This  part  of  the  book  is  of  about  the  end 
of  the  twelfth  century. 

(h)  An  Appendix  to  the  original  Collectar,  which  appears 
to  have  been  made  gradually.  The  original  hand, 
or  one  extremely  like  it,  appears,  with  others,  right 
to  the  end  of  the  Appendix.  The  inclusion  of 
Masses  for  S.  Hugh  and  S.  William  shows  that  this 
Appendix  was  gradually  made  up  to  about  the 
middle  of  the  thirteenth  century.  This  part  I  have 
called  the  "  Appendix." 

1  I  use  the  word  Collectar  loosely  for  want  of  a  better  term.     A  Collectar 
is  properly  a  book  containing  the  Collects  and  Chapters  of  the  Dav  Offices. 

h   ^> 


xn  INTRODUCTION. 

(c)  A  Supplement  prefixed  to  the  volume.  This  contains 
some  of  the  matter  of  the  AppendLx  again,  and  was 
apparently  written  chieily  for  the  purpose  of  having 
these  special  (iilbertine  Masses  in  order.  At  the 
time  of  the  adding  of  this  Supplement  to  the  book 
the  leaves  containing  the  first  part  of  the  Ambrosian 
prayer  were  removed,  and  the  first  part  of  the 
prayer  rewritten  in,  I  think,  the  hand  of  the  writer 
of  the  "  Supplement."  Apparently  as  much 
space  was  not  available  as  was  originally  intended, 
and  the  rewritten  portion  had  therefore  to  be 
crowded  in  in  small  writing.  The  object  in  re- 
writing this  prayer  seems  to  have  been  that  a 
different  version  of  it  might  be  inserted.  This 
section  is,  in  the  judgment  of  Sir  George  Warner, 
before  the  middle  of  the  thirteenth  century. 

The  book  was  apparently  written  originally  for  a  House  of 
Canons  only,  and  then  was  adapted  for  a  house  to  which 
"  Sorores  "  were  added.  This  seems  evident  from  the  fact 
that  in  the  Missa  de  Perseverantia  the  words  et  sororum 
have  been  added  by  an  early  hand,  and  again  in  the  Missa 
pro  defunctis  fratrihus  congregaiionis  we  again  find  an 
insertion  of  the  words  sorores  et  familiares.  And  again,  on  the 
added  fo.  135,  there  is  a  Mass  Pro  femina  defuncta.  But  there 
is  no  evidence  that  any  House  of  Canons  was  refounded  to 
include  "  Sisters  "  also. 

On  the  other  hand,  when  the  Priory  of  S.  Katherine's, 
Lincoln,  was  founded  by  Bishop  Robert  de  Chesney  in  1148, 
he  committed  to  the  charge  of  the  new  Prior  the  care  of 
the  Hospital  of  the  Holy  Sepulchre,  an  institution  for  the 
education  of  children.  This  hospital  seems  to  have  been 
under  the  care  of  "  Sisters "  who  by  the  union  of  the 
two  came  under  the  rule  of  S.  Gilbert.  "  So^  close  w^as 
the  connection  between  them  that  we  find  the  Priory 
frequently  spoken  of  as  the  Hospital  of  S.  Katherine  and 
S.  Sepulchre,  and  the  Prior  as  the  Prior  of  the  Hospital 
of  Lincoln.  It  appears  also  to  have  given  a  more  prominent 
position  to  the  Lay  Brethren  who  had  charge  of  the  Hospital, 
while  the  Canons  were-  properly  occupied  with  the  services  of 

»  R.  E.  G.  Cole,  The  Priory  of  St.  Katherine  and  Hospital  of  the  Holy 
Sepulchre  (Assoc.  Architect.  Societies'  Reports  and  Papers,  xxvi.  Ft.  II 
(1904^.  pp.  264  ff.). 


INTRODUCTION.  xiii 

the  Church.  As  a  separate  body,  in  earlier  times  at  least,  the 
Lay  Brethren  had  their  own  endowments,  equalling  or  exceed- 
ing those  of  the  Canons  ;  and  we  find  mention  of  a  '  Master  of 
the  Hospital  of  S.  Sepulchre,'  who  aj)j)cars  to  have  been  their 
head,  no  doubt  in  subordination  to  the  Prior.  They  were 
assisted  in  their  care  of  the  sick  and  the  children  by  Lay 
Sisters,  who,  under  the  name  of  Sisters  of  S.  Katherine's, 
were  the  frequent  recipients  of  bequests.  ^  They  here  took  the 
place,  though  in  a  lower  rank,  of  the  '  Sanctimoniales  '  or 
professed  nuns,  who  held  so  prominent  a  place  in  the  Gilbertine 
Houses." 

Again,  S.  Gilbert,  in  fixing  the  maximum  number  of 
*'  Moniales  "  in  the  different  liouscs,  for  which  purpose  he 
expressly  includes  with  the  Moniales  the  lay  sisters  (numerus  .  . 
monialium  quibus  sorores  connumeramus)  assigns  to  Lincoln 
twenty  only.^  This  small  number,  which  is  much  less  than 
those  of  the  double  houses  proper,  tends  to  confirm  the  view 
that  these  Sorores  at  S.  Katherine's  w^re  lay  sisters  only, 
but  of  a  standing  different  from  and  superior  to  that  of  the 
ordinary  lay  sisters  of  a  double  house.  Though  then  S. 
Katherine's  was  not  properly  a  double  house,  it  is  clear  that 
this  reference  to  "  Sisters  "  would  cover  S.  Katherine's  as  well 
as  any  strictly  double  house,  while,  on  the  other  hand,  no 
double  house  would  have  had  need  to  alter  the  prayers  of  its 
service  books  by  the  addition  of  the  word  "  sorores,"  since  all 
such  were  double  from  the  date  of  their  foundation. 

In  addition  to  this  evidence  pointing  to  S.  Katherine's  as 
the  owner  of  this  Missal  may  be  adduced  the  fact  that  there  is 
a  Special  Mass  of  S.  Katherine  in  the  prefixed  Supplement. 
This  is  not  by  any  means  conclusive  in  itself,  but  the  whole 
Mass  with  its  choir  parts  is  here  given.  In  another  part  of  the 
book  it  is  true  that  the  Mass  prayers  of  S.  Helen  are  given, 
and  on  a  special  leaf  apparently  inserted  so  as  to  include  them. 
The  Gilbertine  house  of  Mattersey  was  dedicated  to  S.  Helen, 
but  on  the  other  hand  this  house  was  a  House  of  Canons  only, 
and  therefore  does  not  explain  the  insertions. 

The  evidence  is,   I  think,  sufficient  to  conclude   that   this

Isabella  Chawelton,  sister  of  S.  Katherine's,  Lincoln," — Gibbon,  Early 
Lincoln  Wills,  pp.  86,  95,  185.  Cf.  "  To  the  Sisterys  dwellyng  in  Lincoln

xiv  IXTRODUCTION, 

book  was  actually  written  for  tlie  Priory  of  S.  Katherine's  at 
Lincoln. 

The  fact  that  it  has  been  preserved  in  the  Chapter  Library 
does  not  prove  anything.  It  was  there  at  the  time  of  the 
making  of  the  1660  Catalogue  of  MSS.  in  which  the  entry 
'*  Item  Missale  "  cannot  refer  to  any  otlier  MS.  in  the  Library, 
though  it  is  described  as  a  Folio.  There  have  been  no  MSS. 
lost  or  alienated  from  the  Library,  so  far  as  I  am  aware,  since 
that  date.  At  the  period  of  the  Reformation  a  certain  number 
of  MSS.  from  the  flotsam  and  jetsam  of  monastic  libraries 
found  a  linal  resting  place  at  Lmcoln,  and  it  is  likely  enough 
that  among  these  came  this  Missal  from  S.  Katherine's.  There 
are  no  other  S.  Katherine  MSS.  there. 

The  gatherings  of  the  different  parts  of  the  book  are  as 
follows  : — 

(c)  The  Prefixed  Supplement.  P  (wants  i),  IP-V»,  Vpo, 
\TP*\  \'III^.  The  last  gathering  of  four  leaves 
contains  on  the  tirst  three  (ff.  60-62)  the  end  of 
the  prefixed  matter,  with  three  added  Masses ; 
and  the  last.  fo.  63,  contains  the  beginning  of  the 
'*  Ambrosian  '*  prayer  crowded  in  to  tit  the  original 
ending. 

(a)  The   Collectar.     IX«,    X«   (5,  6,  inserted),  XP-XVP, 

XVIPo  (3.  6,  inserted). 

(b)  The  Appendix.     XMIP  (slip  between  3.  4),  XIX^. 

The  Date  of  the  Missal. 

There  are  some  difficulties  connected  with  the  dating  of  the 
earliest  part  of  the  Missal,  the  Collectar,  which  is  the  basis  of 
the  book. 

The  late  Dr.  Bannister  uTote  :  "  The  date  of  the  earlier  part, 
ff.  65  sqq.,  if  judged  by  its  contents,  should  be  before  the 
Canonization  of  S.  Thomas  of  Canterbury;,  11 72,  as  the  Mass 
for  December  29th  is  not  found  in  the  original  hand,  but  is 
inserted  by  a  later  one  at  the  foot  of  fo.  j/"^.  It  is  true  that  if 
this  part  of  the  MS.  were  \mtten  abroad,  and  not  in  England 
(and  to  this  some  slight  witness  is  borne  by  the  script,  the 
imitative  rustic  capitals  for  some  of  the  rubrics  and  the  initial 
letter  in  three  colours  near  the  commencement),  it  might  be 
put  towards  the  end  of  the  centur}-,  a  date  which  seems  more 
suitable  palaeographically  ;  but  any  French  Cistercian  MS.  would 
necessarily  have  included  S.  Thomas  ver}-  shortly  after  his 
death." 


ISTRODUCTIOX,  xv 

I  think  the  book  is  EngHsh  throughout,  and  it  was  probably 
written  by  a  member  of  the  house  where  it  was  first  in  use, 
since  the  original  hand  of  the  Collectar  keeps  on  reappearing, 
and  persists  nearly  to  the  end,  in  the  Appendix  that  was  added 
by  degrees  till  ver^'  nearly  the  middle  of  the  thirteenth  centur^\ 
In  this  case  a  date  not  long  before  the  end  of  the  twelfth 
centur^^  seems  to  be  required  for  the  original  book. 

On  the  other  hand,  there  can,  I  think,  be  no  doubt  that  it 
was  copied  from  a  Cistercian  Missal,  one  or  two  places  being 
left  deliberately  blank  where  a  prayer  differing  from  that  of 
the  Cistercian  rite  was  substituted.  But  this  leaves  the  diffi- 
culty of  the  omission  from  the  text  of  the  service  of  St.  Thomas 
on  December  29th.  The  only  explanation  that  I  can  offer  of 
this  phenomenon  is  that  the  book  was  copied  from  a  Cistercian 
exemplar  of  a  date  anterior  to  the  Canonization  of  S.  Thomas, 
and  that  it  was  only  later  that  the  omission  was  perceived. 
The  small  hand  that  notes  S.  Thomas  at  the  foot  of  the  page 
is,  if  not  contemporary'  with  the  hand  of  the  Collectar,  very 
nearly  so.  A  further  noticeable  feature  in  connection  with 
this  matter  is  that  the  Collect  pro\'ided  in  this  note  for 
S.  Thomas  is  not  that  of  the  familiar  Mass  of  S.  Thomas,  but 
that  proxided  in  the  Common  for  a  Mart\T  Bishop.  In  the 
later  prefixed  Supplement  the  familiar  Collect  appears.  It 
would  seem  that  for  a  time  at  least  S.  Thomas  was  provided 
for  in  the  Gilbert ine  Order  from  the  Common.^ 

The  Appendix  was  of  gradual  formation,  and  I  think  that  the 
extreme  end  of  it  may  have  been  written  even  after  the  addi- 
tion of  the  Supplement  at  the  beginning.  The  Supplement  to 
some  extent  replaces  parts  at  least  of  the  Appendix. 

The  appearance  of  the  Mass  Prayers  of  S.  William  and 
S.  Hugh  towards  the  end  of  the  Appendix  brings  the  date  of  its 
completion  nearly  up  to  the  year  1230,  for  S.  Hugh  was  not 
canonized  till  1220,  and  S.  Wilham  of  York  till  1227. 

The  prefixed  Supplement  is  the  last  part  of  the  book  wxitten. 
Sir  George  Warner  confirms  my  estimate  of  the  date  that  it  is 
towards  the  end  of  the  first  half  of  the  thirteenth  century,  but 
before  1250.  Further  confirmation  of  this  date  is  found  in  the 
fact  that  at  the  end  of  the  Supplement,  among  some  other 
matter,  is  a  Mass  for  S.  Edmund  of  Canterbur\'  in  a  slightly 
later  hand.     S.  Edmund  was  canonized  in  1247,  and  therefore

for  St  Thomas  in  the  Psalter  and  Hours  of  Abbot  Robert  de  Lindseyof  Peter- 
borough (died  1220).     This  book  is  St.  John's  Coll.  Camb.  MS.  Si. 


xvi  INTRODUCTIOS. 

the  inference-  is  that  the  Supplement  projxjr  was  written  before 
this  year. 

The  whole  book  therefore  represents  the  Gilbertine  Missal  in 
its  different  stages,  showing  its  development  from  its  beginnings 
till  the  date  when  it  received  its  final  form,  c.  1250.  A  few 
years  later  the  Gilbertine  Mass  services,  as  app>ears  from  the 
S.  John's  Coll.  MS.,  appear  stereot\'ped  and  fixed.  The  S.  John's 
book  cannot  be  much  later  than  c.  1260. 

2.  S.  John's  Coll.  Camh.  MS.  239  (J.). — The  MS.,  as  has 
been  said,  consists  of  a  Kalendar,  the  Bible,  the  Canon  of  the 
Mass  and  Prefaces,  and  the  Masses  peculiar  to  the  Gilbertine 
Use,  all  in  the  same  hand.  It  is  probably  of  a  date  slightly 
before  1265,  for  on  the  end  fly-leaf  are  written  some  of  Joachim's 
prophecies,  among  which  occurs  one  which  foretells  the  return 
of  all  Greece  to  the  Roman  obedience  in  the  3'ear  1265.  Since 
this  prophecy  was  not  fulfilled,  I  think  we  may  conclude  that 
it  was  written  here  before  the  year  in  question,  and  tliis  places 
the  date  of  the  book  before  1265.  The  hand  is  of  about  that 
date.  There  is  not  a  sign  of  the  house  or  individual  to  which 
this  book  belonged. 

The  general  contents  of  the  book  point  to  its  probabh'  being 
simply  a  norm  or  standard  text  of  the  Gilbertine  Kalendar  and 
Mass  services,  and  never  intended  for  use  at  the  altar.  It 
represents  the  final  form  of  the  Lincoln  book  in  a  somewhat 
shorter  form,  being  not  so  rich  in  its  Common,  and  lacking 
those  Masses  of  the  Lincoln  book  which  are  of  local  interest. 

The  contents  of  the  liturgical  portion  of  this  book  is  as

INTRODUCTION. 


Every  one  of  these  Masses  is  to  be  found  somewhere  in  the 
Lincoln  Missal,  with  some  difference  of  title  in  some  of  the 
votive  Masses,  yet  the  number  of  Masses  peculiar  to  the  Use 
is  less  here  than  in  the  Lincoln  book.  It  would  be  interesting 
to  know  with  what  other  book  this  collection  of  J  would  be 
used.  In  L  the  Collectar  is  pure  Cistercian,  with  but  a  few 
deliberate  alterations.  I  think  it  is  likely  that  later  on  the  local 
Use  would  be  followed  with  the  exceptions  here  provided  for. 

The  relation  existing  between  the  Lincoln  book  and  J 
is  best  shown  by  setting  out  the  contents  of  the  two  side  by 
side.  I  have  here  therefore  set  in  parallel  columns  the  Collectar 
of  the  Lincoln  book,  showing  also  the  relation  between  this 
original  portion  and  the  Appendix  which  forms  an  addition, 
and  also  the  Supplement  which  is  partly  of  the  nature  of  an 
addition  and  partly  of  a  revised  edition.  It  will  be  seen  that 
J  corresponds  with  the  completed  contents  of  L.

(Fidelium) 

3.  The  Office  0]  S.  Gilbert. — This  offtce  is  contained  in  Bodl. 
MS.  Digby  36,  a  volume  which  contains  beside  this  office  the 
statutes  of  the  Gilbertine  Order.  The  book  is  of  the  fourteenth 
century.     I  do  not  know  of  any  other  text  of  this  office. 


4.  The  Ordinale. — This  is  contained  in  a  MS.  belonging  to 
Pembroke  College,  Cambridge,  No.  226.  It  is  entitled  De 
ecclesiasticis  officiis  secundum  ritus  canoniconim  ordinis  de 
ssemp[ingham],  and  is  the  Ordinale  of  the  Order.  The  volume 
would  seem  to  have  been  written  in  the  first  half  of  the  fifteenth 
century.  It  is  in  two  hands.  The  book  has  been  much  used 
and  in  places  the  bottom  of  the  leaves  is  illegible  or  hardly 


xxiv  INTRODUCTION 

legible  from  much  thumbing.  It  has  also  been  subjected  to 
a  considerable  amount  of  alteration,  and  it  would  seem  that 
the  exemplar  from  which  this  copy  was  made  was  of  an  early 
date ;  the  Feast  of  Corpus  Ghristi  not  being  part  of  the  original 
contents,  but  inserted  in  a  different  hand. 

There  is  nothing  to  show  to  what  house  tliis  Ordinal  belonged. 
The  second  of  the  two  documents  inserted  between  the  Tem- 
porale  and  the  Sanctorale  apparently  applies  to  the  whole 
Order,  as  does  the  former.  If  it  only  applies  to  one  house 
that  house  must  manifestly  be  Sempringham  (or  Sempingham, 
as  it  is  usually  spelt)  itself,  for  the  John  Viscount  Beaumont 
for  whose  benefit  the  office  was  prescribed  was  a  benefactor  of 
the  mother  house.  His  will  is  still  extant,^  dated  September  8th, 
1396,  in  which  he  directs  that  he  "is  to  be  buried  in  Sem- 
pringham Church  near  the  body  of  my  most  honoured  lord,  my 
Father  "  ;  and  he  leaves  "  to  Sempringham  Priory  the  little 
cross  made  of  our  Lord's  Cross."  The  antiphons  for  the  Feast 
of  Relics  show  that  among  those  relics  was  one  of  Holy  Cross  ; 
is  this  the  relic  in  question  ?  Another  Beaumont,  Henry 
Lord  of  Folkingham,2  also  directs  in  his  will  (1413)  that  he  is 
to  be  buried  in  Sempringham  Convent  Church. 

There  remains  one  small  piece  of  evidence  that  might  serve 
to  identify  the  book  with  some  particular  house,  and  that 
consists  of  a  scribbling  at  the  end  of  the  book  by  a  hand  which 
would  appear  to  be  of  the  early  sixteenth  century.  There  four 
names  occur,  all  in  the  same  hand,  one  under  another  : 

Thomas  Smythe  can.  &  subprior  (P)^  istius  domus 
Johannes  Symson 
Ry chard  Symson 
Jorge  Benton. 

The  name  of  Richard  Symson  occurs  in  connection  with 
the  surrender  of  Ellerton  Priory,  which  surrender  was  actually 
made  per  me  Ricardum  Symson.^  Possibly,  then,  the  book 
belonged  to  Ellerton,  but  the  name  is  too  common  a  one  for 
much  reliance  to  be  placed  on  w^hat  may  easily  be  a  mere 
coincidence. 

Among  other  scribblings  appears  the  name  John  Jakson.

*  Id,,  ibid.,  p.  III. 

3  This  word  is  not  at  all  certain.  It  is  abbreviated  and  written  ^,  which 
should  stand  for  sey\uns],  but  this  seems  unsuitable.  "  Frater  "  is  impossible, 
and  so  I  suggest  subprior.

INTRODUCTION.  xxv 

The  Gilbertine  Use. 

When  S.  Gilbert  first  founded  his  Order  he  wished  to  hand  it 
over  to  the  charge  of  the  great  Order  of  Citeaux .  The  Cistercian 
Order,  however,  not  having  at  this  time  undertaken  the  charge 
of  women,  refused  to  entertain  (Gilbert's  suggestion,  and  as  a 
result  his  Order  was  established  independently  under  his  own 
supervision  as  Master.  We  are  told  in  the  Vita  that  he  imposed 
different  rules  on  his  Canons  and  his  Nuns,  on  the  former  the 
rule  of  S.  Augustine,  and  on  the  latter  that  of  S.  Benedict. 
He  did  not,  however,  impose  these  rules  exactly  in  their 
original  form,  but  where  he  thought  that  he  could  improv^e  on 
them  from  other  sources,  he  did  not  hesitate  to  do  so.  The 
words  that  relate  this  plan  of  his  seem,  however,  to  include 
more  than  the  actual  rules  and  to  cover  his  liturgical  rite.  We 
are  told  "  a  multarum  ecclesiarum  et  monasteriorum  statutis 
et  consuetudinibus,  quasi  flores  pulcherrimos  excerpsit,  collegit 
et  praeelegit."  ^  The  inclusion  of  the  rules  and  customs  of 
churches  would  seem  to  cover  questions  of  liturgy.  His  Use 
shows  an  eclecticism  that  is  accounted  for  by  this  explanation 
of  his  practice  when  founding  liis  Order  and  forming  his  Rule. 

The  Use  is,  generally  speaking,  Cistercian.  The  body  of  the 
Missal  is  evidently  copied  directly  from  the  Cistercian  Rite. 
The  rubrics  in  the  Missal  and  some  of  the  Ordinances  in  the 
Ordinale  are  taken  directly  from  the  Consuetudinarium  of  the 
Cistercian  Order,  and  are  in  some  cases  word  for  word  the  same. 
Even  we  find  the  word  "  Abbas  "  so  taken  over  from  the  Cister- 
cian books,  though,  strictly  speaking,  the  head  of  the  Gilbertine 
Order  was  not  an  Abbot,  but  a  Master,  and  the  heads  of  houses 
Priors.  On  the  other  hand,  there  was  even  at  the  first  a 
clearly  indicated  intention,  in  places  at  least,  to  depart  from 
the  Cistercian  exemplar.  Thus  we  find  that  on  Fer.  V.  after 
the  Third  Sunday  in  Lent,  the  original  copyist  has  left  blank 
the  space  for  the  first  three  prayers,  and  these  prayers  have 
been  filled  in  by  a  different  hand,  and  are  not  the  prayers  of 
the  Cistercian  Rite.  Again,  on  the  Vigil  of  the  Ascension, 
space  has  been  left  by  the  copyist  for  the  Collect  and  Post- 

1  The  whole  passage  runs:  ":  .  .  .  Moniahbus  regulam  beati  Benedict!, 
clericis  vero  regulam  S.  Augustini  tenendam  proponens  ;  omnibus  autem 
Christi  et  sanctorum  exempla  et  evangelicam,  apostolicamque  doctrinam 
annuntians.  .  .  .  Et  quia  pro  locis  et  temporibus  personisque  ex  causis 
incidentibus  variatur  et  mutari  oportet  cura  institutorum,  ea  quae  minus 
in  illis  regulis  invenit  ad  sic  datam  normam  sufficientia,  a  multarum  ecclesiarum 
et  monasteriorum  statutis  et  consuetudinibus,  quasi  flores  pulcherrimos  excerp- 
sit collegit  et  praeelegit  quae  magis  necessaria  et  competentiora  sic  infirmatis 
hominibus  iudicavit.

xxvi  INTRODUCTION. 

communion,  and  here  again  they  have  been  written  in  by 
another  liand  and  are  different  from  the  corresponding  prayers 
of  the  Cistercian  Rite. 

Again,  a  generous  Commune  Sanctorum  is  provided  in  the 
Gilbertine  Use,  a  deliberate  variation  from  the  Use  of  Citeaux, 
which  had  no  Common.  But  it  is  the  prefixed  Supplement  in 
which  is  gathered  together  chiefly  the  body  of  Masses  which 
have  characteristics  peculiar  to  the  use  of  the  Gilbertine  Order. 
In  this  series  of  specially  Gilbertine  Masses  we  have  much  in 
common  with  the  Cistercian  Use,  but  on  the  other  hand  a 
considerable  amount  of  variation  showing  great  eclecticism 
as  to  the  sources  from  which  the  various  forms  were  derived. 

Thus  the  Alleluia  Verse  and  Epistle  of  S.  Thomas  of  Canter- 
bury, the  Office  Psalm  of  the  Ascension,  the  Mass,of  S.  Gilbert, 
the  second  Alleluia  Verse  of  the  Commemoration  of  S.  Paul, 
the  Communion  of  S.  Katherine,  the  original  Secret  and  Post- 
communion  of  S.  Nicholas  (for  which  others  have  been  sub- 
stituted by  another  hand  in  the  margin)  and  the  Mass  De 
Perseverantia  I  have  been  unable  to  find  elsewhere,  and  appear 
to  be  unique  ;  and  also  I  have  not  found  elsewhere  the  sequences 
Quam  speciosi  and  Ortus  sol  ;  while,  so  far  as  I  am  aware, 
the  sequences  Inclita  (St.  Catherine)  and  Interni  festi  (St. 
Jerome)  occur  in  no  other  English  Use. 

The  Mass  Per  annum  of  S.  Gilbert  which  occurs  in  J  and 
the  Ordinal  is  very  similar  to  but  not  identical  with  the  Mass 
of  S.  Gilbert  in  the  York  Use,  which  is  that  of  the  Common  of 
a  Confessor  not  a  Bishop. 

On  S.  Thomas's  Day  the  Gilbertine  Mass  agrees  with  SH 
against  CY,  etc.,  for  the  Officium,  with  YH  Whit.,  etc., 
against  CS  for  the  Collect,  with  YHW  Whit.,  etc.,  against  C 
for  the  Gospel. 

So  on  S.  Michael's  Day  the  Lectio  is  that  of  SYH  against  C. 

But  perhaps  the  Mass  of  S.  Katherine  shows  best  the  eclecti- 
cism of  the  Use. 

Offic.  With  HW  against  CSY,  etc. 

Coll.  HY  against  CSW. 

Lect.  H  against  CYS. 

Grad.  With  Y  against  CSH. 

Offert.  SYH  against  C. 

Postc.  SYW  against  C,  etc. 
and  the  sequence  Inclita  alone  against  all. 

Again,  in  the  Supplement  is  to  be  seen  the  process  of  develop- 
ment even  during  the  time  covered  by  the  wTiting  of  the  book 


INTRODUCTION.  xxvii 

itself.  Thus  we  find  prayers  substituted  in  the  Supplement 
for  those  already  sup])lied  in  the  old  portion,  the  Collectar 
proper.  For  instance,  for  S.  Mary  Magdalene,  who  in  the 
Collectar  is  served  by  the  Common,  the  Supplement  supplies  a 
new  set  of  prayers,  the  Collect  which  agrees  with  SYH 
against  C,  the  Secret  with  H\V  Durh.,etc.,  against  C,  and  the 
Post-communion  with  H  Durh.,  etc.,  against  C.  Again,  on  the 
Assumption  a  new  Collect  agreeing  with  SYH,  etc.,  is  supplied 
in  the  place  of  that  of  C,  which  appears  for  this  feast  in  the 
Collectar.  In  the  same  way  in  the  case  of  the  Feast  of  the 
Nativity  of  the  Virgin  Mary  a  new  Collect  agreeing  with  tliat 
of  SYH  Durh.,  etc.,  is  supplied  for  the  old  one  of  C  which 
occurs  in  the  Collectar. 

Perhaps  the  most  interesting  feature  of  the  book  is  the 
prayers  at  Communion.  Here  originally  the  Cistercian  Rite 
was  followed  and  the  order  of  the  prayers  was  : 

Domine  ihesu  christe. 

Corpus  tuam  domini  nostri  ihesu  christi. 

Placeat  tibi  sancta  trinitas. 

But  by  a  very  early  hand,  probably  contemporar}',  the  rare 
prayer  Aginms  tibi  deo  patri  has  been  inserted  at  the  bottom 
of  the  page  in  small  writing.  This  has  been  erased  (probably 
because  of  the  smallness  of  the  writing)  and  rewritten  in  the 
right-hand  margin  rather  later  in  a  larger  hand.  But  this 
proving  unsatisfactory,  two  leaves  have  been  inserted  and  the 
whole  series  of  prayers  rewritten  : 

Domine  ihesu  christe. 
Agimus  tibi  deo  patri. 
Corpus  domini  nostri. 
Sanguis  domini  nostri. 
Placeat  tibi  sancta  trinitas. 

The  rewritten  pages  are  manifestly  for  the  sake  of  including 
the  prayer  Agimus  tibi  deo  patri,  but  it  is  noticeable  that 
the  opportunity  has  been  taken  to  insert  also  the  form  of 
administration  for  the  chalice.  The  administration  forms  also 
are  peculiar  for  their  use  of  the  word  maneat.  This  I  have 
found  elsewhere  only  in  the  Bristol  Augustinian  Missal  (and  it 
is  to  be  remembered  that  the  Canons  of  S.  Gilbert  followed  the 
rule  of  S.  Augustine),  and  in  one  French  book.  The  prayer 
Domine  ihesu  christe  has  also  its  singulars  changed  to  the  plural. 
Here  again  J  represents  the  final  development  of  these  prayers. 
They   are   there   exactly   as   in   these    inserted   leaves.     The 

c    2 


xxviii  INTRODUCTION, 

(iillx'rtine  Use  seems  to  have  become  fixed  in  its  entirety  by 
the  time  of  J,  and  its  latest  developments  were,  it  would  seem, 
and  indeed  as  was  natural,  in  the  direction  of  preferring  local 
English  usages  over  the  Cistercian  from  which  it  was  originally 
derived. 

But  in  even  the  last  development,  as  represented  by  the 
Supplement,  the  influence  is  chiefly  Cistercian.  In  this  part 
it  is  difficult  to  see  any  other  very  marked  influence.  If  an3^ 
it  is  perhaps  there  are  signs  of  a  tendency  to  the  York  Use 
but  even  so,  very  slight.  Wlien  we  come  to  the  Ordinate  we 
hnd  this  same  eclecticism  evident — sometimes  it  seems  to 
approximate  to  York,  from  time  to  time  we  find  it  in  singular 
agreement  with  H,  but  all  through  manifesting  a  very  decided 
spirit  of  independence.  And  here  we  must  notice  that  in 
some  respects  at  least  there  was  not  uniformity  within  the 
Order  itself.  This  is  expressly  stated  to  be  the  case  in  Pro- 
cessions. Of  the  Palm  Sunday  Procession  we  are  told  (p.  zy) 
hec  processio  a  diiiersis  diuerse  anteni  in  ordine  nostra  agitur ; 
and  again  of  the  Rogation-tide  processions  (p.  49),  Processiones 
rogacionum  uarie  fiunt  in  domibus  canonicorum  secundum 
consuetiidincs  ct  situs  locorum.  This  latter  statement  that 
local  use  influenced  different  houses  is  of  importance. 

So  far  as  wc  can  judge  from  the  Ordinalc  the  Breviary  of  the 
Gilbcrtines  must  liave  differed  considerably  from  the  Cistercian 
Use.  The  hymns  differ  very  much.  Yet  the  Ordinate  was 
composed  with  the  Cistercian  Consuetudinary  as  a  ground  work, 
for  as  in  the  rubrics  of  the  Missal  there  are  some  passages  of 
the  Ordinate  that  are  taken  word  for  word  from  the  Cistercian 
Consuetudinary.  It  may  be  worth  while  to  note  that  the 
Gilbertine  Ordinate  is  quite  independent  of  the  Cistercian 
Ordinariiim,  which  seems  to  be  of  a  later  date.  But  an  affinity 
to  the  York  Use  is  quite  noticeable  when  we  come  to  the  contents 
of  the  Kalendar.  Still,  as  will  be  seen,  there  is  a  very  marked 
eclecticism  in  the  Gilbertine  Use  as  seen  in  these  books.  There 
is  an  appreciable  amount  of  matter  that  appears  to  be  unique 
and  so  would  appear  to  owe  its  origin  to  S.  Gilbert — however, 
where  there  is  agreement  with  English  Uses  against  the  Cistercian 
Use  which  dominates  the  rite,  even  there  the  eclecticism  is 
so  marked  as  to  give  the  impression  that  the  Editor  has  picked 
one  prayer  from  one  source,  another  from  another,  and  so  on, 
exactly,  indeed,  as  we  are  told  expressly  that  S.  Gilbert  did 
when  it  seemed  good  to  him  in  the  composition  of  his  Rule. 
But  I  think  another  consideration  enters  in  here.     S.  Gilbert 


INTRODUCTION.  xxix 

was  born  in  Lincolnshire,  and  beneficed  in  Lincolnshire  all 
his  life.  Except  for  a  few  years  during  which  he  completed 
his  education  somewhere  in  France,  he  was  liv^ing  in  Lincoln- 
shire. And  yet,  furtlier,  for  a  period  of  some  nine  years  he  was 
living  in  the  household  of  the  Bishop  of  Lincoln,  serving  as  a 
Clerk  to  the  Bishop,  first  with  Robert  Bloet  and  then  with 
Alexander.  VVe  are  expressly  told  in  his  Life  that  he  was  not 
a  man  of  very  much  intellectual  ability.  The  natural  conclusion 
that  one  would  draw  from  this  is  that  in  the  formation  of  his 
service  books  he  would  follow  naturally  and  instinctively  the 
service  books  with  which  all  his  life  he  would  be  familiar,  that 
is,  those  of  the  great  diocese  of  Lincoln.  It  seems  possible, 
therefore,  that  the  apparently  unique  forms  which  we  find  here 
and  there  in  the  Gilbertine  books  are  Lincoln  in  origin,  and  to 
the  same  source,  too,  may  perhaps  be  referred  the  combinations 
of  prayers  and  forms  which  in  the  light  of  extant  books  seem 
to  be  purely  eclectic.  There  is  nothing  to  prove  or  disprove 
this  possible  obligation  to  the  "  Use  "  of  Lincoln,  though  the 
circumstances  would  point  naturally  to  this  being  the  case. 
In  the  absence  of  any  Lincoln  service  books  theories  only  are 
possible  as  to  the  Use  of  Lincoln. 

The  Kalendar. 

The  Cistercian  Use  has  no  Common.  S.  Gilbert  provided  a 
Common  for  his  Use.  At  the  beginning  of  the  Collectar, 
accordingly,  is  found  an  index  of  Saints  who  were  to  be  provided 
for  out  of  the  Common  services.  This  is  written  in  a  different 
hand  from  that  of  the  copyist  of  the  Collectar,  but  contemporary 
with  it.  The  original  saints  in  this  index  are  all  in  the 
Cistercian  Kalendar,  but  a  number  of  names,  mainly  English, 
have  been  added  in  yet  another  hand  more  or  less  contempoiary. 
At  the  end  of  the  Ordinale  there  is  a  similar  index.  The  two 
are  given  side  by  side  below.  Those  who  have  been  added 
in  the  Lincoln  book  are  marked  with  a  (f).  There  are  no 
dates  given  in  either  book,  and  those  given  below  are  the  dates 
usually  assigned.

Hibaldi  Ep.  et  Conf. 


The  original  list  in  the  Index  of  the  Missal  is  purely  Cister- 
cian. The  Index  of  the  Ordinal  shows  a  later  development. 
It  will  be  noticed  that  for  certain  saints  who  have  a  Proper  in 
the  Missal  the  Ordinal  provides  from  the  Common,  e.g.,  June  28, 
S.  Leo  ;  Jul 37  2,  SS.  Processus  and  Martinianus  ;  August  6,  SS. 
Felicissimus  and  Agapitus,  etc.  ;  while  on  the  other  hand  some 
saints  who  in  the  ^lissal  depend  on  the  Common  have  acquired 
a  proper  office  in  the  Ordinal,  such  as  S.  Augustine  of  Canter- 
bury, who  in  the  Ordinal  has  even  his  own  procession  (May  26). 

But  the  most  interesting  feature  of  the  Index  of  the  Missal 
is  the  added  names.     These  are  : 

S.  Edward  K/and  C. 
S.  Wulfstan. 
S.  Blasius. 
S  Cedde. 


INTRODUCTION.  xxxiii 

S.  Edward  K.  and  M. 

S.  Cuthbert. 

S.  Guthlac. 

S.  EJphcge  Abp. 

S.  Clctus. 

S.  Erkenwald. 

S.  John  of  Beverley. 

Translation  of  St.  Andrew.  Translation  of  St.  Nicholas. 

S.  Dunstan. 

S.  Augustine  of  Canterbury 

S.  Bede. 

S.  William  of  York. 

S.  Botulf. 

S.  Etheldreda. 

S.  Swithun. 

S.  Mildred. 

S.  Kenelm  K.M. 

S.  Margaret. 

S.  Oswald. 

S.  H\lda. 

S.  Ethelwold. 

S.  Wilfrid. 

S.  Frideswide. 

S.  Edmund  K.M. 

S.  Linus. 

The  list  is  noticeably  English,  and  some  of  the  names  doubtless 
owe  their  inclusion  in  the  list  to  their  general  fame,  as  perhaps 
S.  Dunstan.  S.  Elphege,^  S.  Ethelwold,  S.  Cuthbert.  But 
S.  Ethelwold's  activities  in  the  restoration  of  the  monastic 
life  after  the  Danish  devastation  extended  into  Lincolnshire. 
Some  of  the  names,  such  as  SS.  John  of  Beverley,  Bede,  Hilda, 
and  Archbishop  William  point  definitely  to  York.  Of  these 
names  SS.  Bede  and  Hilda  occur  only  in  the  Missal  and  Ordinal. 
S.  William  the  Archbishop  is  in  the  Kalendar  of  J,  and 
S.  John  of  Beverley  is  in  the  Kalendar  of  J  and  in  the  Kalendar 
of  Lincoln  Cathedral.  But  the  geographical  position  of 
Lincolnshire  with  respect  to  Yorkshire  is,  I  think,  sufficient  to 
account  for  these  names.     Other  names  are  more  difficult  to 

^  But  the  name  in  the  Ordinal  of  Elphegus  Ep.C.  on  March  12,  who  is 
Bishop  of  Winchester  of  that  name,  perhaps  points  to  some  Winchester 
influence  somewhere.  This  Elphege  was  known  as  Elphege  the  Bald,  and  died 
in  951.     He  occurs  on  this  day  in  the  Kalendar  of  Cott.  Vitell.  E.  xviii. 


xxxiv  INTRODUCTION. 

account  for,  such  as  S.  Wulfstan  ;    but  he  is  in  the  Lincoln 
Kalendar. 

On  the  other  hand,  the  names  of  S.  Cedde  (for  some  time 
Bishop  of  the  parts  of  Lindsey),  S.  Guthlac,  S.  Erkenvvald 
(born  in  Lindsey),  S.  Botulf  are  unmistakable  in  their  Lincoln 
connections.  S.  Fridesvvide  may  be  included  among  the 
Lincoln  saints  since  Oxford  was  in  the  diocese.  S.  Edward, 
the  King  and  Martyr,  is  in  the  Missal,  the  Ordinal  and  the 
Lincoln  Cathedral  Kalendar  ;  S.  Edmund  the  King  in  all  three 
Gilbertine  books  ;  S.  Oswald  is  in  J,  the  Missal  and  Lincoln 
Kalendar  ;  S.  Kenelm  in  the  Missal,  the  Ordinal,  and  Lincoln. 

When  we  turn  to  the  Ordinal  we  find  two  extra  names  again 
definitely  connected  with  Lincolnshire,  S.  Eadburga  (Ethel- 
burga)  and  S.  Hibald.  The  former  of  these  w^as  born  in 
Lindsey  and  was  sister  of  Erkenwald.  Of  the  latter  little  is  known 
beyond  the  reference  in  Bede.  He  was  an  Abbot  somewhere  in 
Lincolnshire.!  He  is  in  the  Ordinal  described  as  Bishop  and 
Confessor.  The  Ordinal  would  seem  to  give  him  the  date 
December  14.  This  is  one  of  the  several  dates  assigned  to 
this  shadowy  saint.  Is  it  possible  that  there  were  two  Hibalds 
whose  days  were  commemorated,  a  Bishop  and  an  Abbot  ? 
I  think  the  description  in  the  Ordinal  is  a  mistake — Hibald 
the  Abbot  has  four  dedications  in  Lincolnshire  and  none 
elsewhere,  and,  like  Botulf,  has  given  his  name  to  a  place, 
Hibaldstow. 

The  Ordinal  gives  us  one  more  piece  of  information.  The 
Feast  of  Relics  was  between  October  19-28.  The  date  of  the 
York  Feast  was  October  19,  that  of  Lincoln  being  on  July  10. 
The  importance  of  the  Translation  of  S.  Andrew  on  August  9 
is  noticeable.  This  Feast,  however,  we  find  a  Double  at 
Lincoln  in  the  fifteenth  century.  It  may  be  noticed,  when 
w^e  remember  that  the  rule  of  the  Gilbertine  Canons  was 
that  of  S.  Augustine,  that  in  the  Kalendars  of  these 
Augustinian  books,  the  Hanley  Castle  Missal  (C.V.L.  M.S. 
Kk.  2,  6),  the  Holyrood  Ordinate  (F.  C.  Eeles,  1916)  and 
the  Liber  Memorandorum  Ecclesiae  de  Bernewelle  (J.  W.  Clark, 
1907),  the  Translations  of  S.  Andrew  and  of  S.  Nicholas 
appear  in  the  Kalendar  on  August  9.     This  Feast  is  also  found 


1  In  the  catalogue  of  English  saints,  Die  Heiligen  Englands,  ed.  by  G. 
Liebermann,  saint  Higebold  is  said  to  have  been  buried  in  Cesesege  by  the 
river  Oncel.  This  place  Liebermann  identifies  with  Kelsey  (which  is  near 
Hibaldstow),  and  the  river  is  the  Ancholme.  Also  in  B.M.  Royal  MS.  2  A. 
XX  there  is  an  Or  alio  sancti  Hugbaldi  abbatis. 


INTRODUCTION.  xxxv 

in  the  Hours  and  Psalter  supposed  to  have  belonged  to 
Rob.  de  Lindesey,  Abbot  of  Peterborough  (S.  Joh.  Coll.  Camb. 
MS.  8i). 

When  we  turn  to  the  Kalendar  of  J,  I  think  that  the  same 
characteristics  are  to  be  observed  belonging  to  it,  as  to  the 
liturgical  contents  of  J.  It  is  a  standard  Kalendar,  and  so 
perhaps  we  can  account  for  the  absence  of  the  Feast  of  the 
Translation  of  S.  Andrew  and  S.  Nicholas,  of  SS.  Erkenwald, 
Kenelm,  Hylda,  and  Helena. 

Judging  from  the  silence  of  the  Ordinal  as  to  S.  Hugh 
(whose  day,  however,  must  have  been  kept),  not  much  seems 
to  have  been  made  of  him  by  the  Gilbertine  Order.  ^  On  the 
other  hand,  so  far  as  I  can  discover  S.  Gilbert  was  not  com- 
memorated at  all  in  the  Lincoln  Cathedral  Kalendar. 

I  append  here  parallel  Kalendars  of  J,  the  Missal,  the  Ordinal, 
Lincoln  Cathedral,  and  the  Kalendar  of  the  Horae  belonging 
to  Christ's  College,  Cambridge  (MS.  6)  of  the  early  years  of  the 
fourteenth  century,  which  is  markedly  Lincolnshire,  or  at  least 
East  Anglian  in  its  features. 

Of  these  the  Ordinal&  Kalendar  is  compiled  from  references 
in  the  text  and  the  Index.  It  is  manifestly  incomplete — for 
example,  S.  Hugh  and  the  Invention  of  the  Cross  are  passed 
over  in  silence.  The  Kalendar  of  Lincoln  Cathedral  is  still 
more  imperfect.  It  is  derived  from  the  Re  and  Ve  Rolls  of 
the  Cathedral."^  None  of  these  is  complete  for  the  whole 
year,  and  they  are  chiefly  of  the  seventeenth  century,  retaining, 
however,  it  would  seem,  most  of  the  names  of  the  unreformed 
Kalendar. 


1  This  omission  of  S.  Hugh  in  the  Ordinal  may,  however,  be  explained  by 
the  fact  that  this  text  was,  I  think,  copied  from  a  much  older  one.  If  the 
exemplar  was  of  a  date  near  that  of  the  founding  of  the  Order,  of  course 
S.  Hugh's  name  would  not  occur.  On  such  an  hypothesis  I  do  not  think  that 
the  failure  to  insert  his  name  later  presents  much  difficulty. 

2  The  Re[cedendi]  et  Ve[niendi]  Rolls  were  those  in  which  the  "  going 
down  "  and  "  coming  up  "  of  the  Prebendaries  out  of  and  into  residence  was 
recorded.

INTRODUCTION.  Iv 

A  word  of  explanation  is  necessary  as  to  the  method  I 
have  followed  in  printing  the  texts.  In  spelling  and  punctua- 
tion I  have  followed  the  original,  even  where,  as  often  in  the 
Ordinale,  it  is  inconsistent.  All  contracted  words  are  expanded 
in  full.  This  is  of  importance  only  with  regard  to  the  headings 
in  the  Prefixed  Supplement  of  the  Missal.  I  have  expanded 
Gr.  into  Gradiiale,  though  this  word  is  never  written  in  full. 
Also  in  this  section  I  have  expanded  Co.  or  Com.  into  the  rare 
form  Communiiim.  This  word  again  is  never  written  in  full, 
but  since  Post  Commiinium  in  this  section  four  times  occurs  in 
full,  it  would  appear  that  the  writer  used  the  form  Communium 
instead  of  the  usual  and  correct  Communio.  This  form  also 
occurs  in  full  on  the  inserted  f.  130.  In  the  other  parts  of  the 
Missal  the  form  is  Communio.  The  prayers,  psalms,  hymns, 
etc.,  of  which  the  first  few  words  are  given  in  the  Ordinale, 
I  have  printed  in  italics  for  the  sake  of  convenience.  All 
through  the  stops  and  spelling  are  given  as  they  appear  in  the 
originals. 

It  remains  for  me  to  thank  the  Master  and  Fellows  of 
Pembroke  College,  Cambridge,  for  the  loan  of  their  MS.  of 
the  Ordinale  to  me  at  Lincoln,  and  in  particular  to  Mr.  E.  H. 
Minns,  Librarian  of  Pembroke,  for  his  kindness  in  looking  up 
doubtful  points  in  the  MS.  after  it  had  returned  to  Cambridge, 
during  the  process  of  printing.  Also  my  grateful  thanks  are 
due  to  the  Honorary  Secretary  of  H.B.S.  for  advice  and  criti- 
cisms during  the  preparation  of  the  book,  and  to  Mr.  Gambier- 
Parry,  who  with  Mr.  Wilson  has  been  at  the  labour  of  reading 
and  correcting  the  proofs.  May  I  acknowledge,  too,  here  my 
indebtedness  to  Miss  Rose  Graham  for  information  on  the  early 
history  of  the  Order. 

Perhaps  I  may  add,  that  owing  to  the  troublous  times  I 
constantly  had  to  lay  aside  my  work  on  this  Rite,  sometimes 
for  long  periods,  since  one  MS.  was  for  the  time  being  inac- 
cessible, and  so  had  to  pick  up  the  threads  again  as  best  I 
might.  This  fact  may  perhaps  be  put  forward  to  excuse,  so 
far  as  may  be,  some  at  least  of  the  deficiencies  which  may  be 
found  in  these  volumes.

De  tercia  ehdemoda  aduentus.    • 

Tercia  ebdemoda  aduentus  per  singulos  dies  habet  proprias 
antiphonas  in  laudibus  que  habentur  eciam  in  quarta  ebdemoda 
usque  ad  uigiliam  natalis  domini.  Et  in  tercia  ebdemoda 
celebratur  legitimum  ieiunium  feria  quarta  et  sexta  et  sabbato  . 
hiis  tribus  diebus  ad  matutinas  euangelia  pronunciantur. 
Responsoria  propria  cantantur  .  Sed  non  sic  in  quarta  ebdemoda  . 
In  illis  uero  diebus  leiuniorum  oraciones  proprie  de  leiunio 
scil.  Festina  &c.  Ad  matutinas  et  ad  omnes  horas  diei  et  ad 
uesperas  dicentur  et  nulla  sit  mencio  de  dominica  nee  eciam 
ad  missam.  Sabbato  autem  ad  uesperas  dicetur  collecta  de 
dominica  /  [adujentus  et  non  de  leiunio  .  Feria  quinta  [fo.  4b 
legantur  sermones  sancti  Augustini  ad  matutinas  scil.  Legimus 
sanctum  Moysen  .  et  uos  inquam  conuenio  .  0  .  iudei.  Eodem 
die  dicetur  collecta  de  precedente  dominica  ad  matutinas  et 
ad  omnes  horas  diei  et  ad  uesperas.  Sabbato  ad  missam  legan-

NOTES 


NOTE. 

I  have  used  for  the  purposes  of  comparison  in  the  notes  only  the 
three  great  Enghsh  Uses  of  Sarum,  York,  and  Hereford  ;  and  the 
Cistercian  Use  that  is  ultimately  the  basis  of  the  Gilbertine  Rite.  These 
texts  are  signified  by  the  following  symbols  : 

S  The  Sarum  Breviary. 

Y  The  York  Breviary. 

H  The  Hereford  Breviary. 

C.  Brev.     The  Cistercian  Breviary. 

C.C.  The  Cistercian  Consuetudinary  (In  P.  Guignard,  Les 

Monuments  primitifs  de  la  regie  cistercienne. 
Analecta  Divionensia,  1878). 

C.  Ord.  The  Cistercian  Ordinal.  (A  paper  MS.  of  the  fifteenth 
century  in  the  possession  of  F.  C.  Eeles,  Esq.  It 
is  not  of  much  importance  as  it  appears  to  be  a  later 
compilation  based  on  the  Consuetudinary.) 

Chr.  Christ's   Coll.   Camb.   MS.   6.      Horae  B.V.M.  of  the 

beginning  of  the  fourteenth  century

p.  33.      De  missa  post  mandatum.    The  C.  Ord.  is  in  general  agreement, 
Sicut  in  natali  unius  apostoli.     So  C.C. 

In  C.C.  the  order  of  proceedings  seems  to  be  different.  There 
is  first  a  Missa  matntinalis,  at  which  all  communicate  ;  and 
a  Missa  de  dominica,  at  which  none  communicate,  and  a 
Missa  pro  defunctis.  While  None  is  being  sung  the  poor  are 
led  to  the  cloisters.  After  this  mandatum  comes  the  man- 
datum  conueyitus,  and  then  the  altars  are  stripped. 

The  details  of  the  Reservation  of  the  Blessed  Sacrament 
are  almost  exactly  the  same  in  C.C.  Compare  the  Rubrics 
of  the  Missal.

terce,    there    is    a    procession    circa  claustrum,   singing    the 

seven  psalms.

p.  86.      Omnium  sanctorum. 

Of  the  benedictions,  the  third  occurs  in  S  for  Ail  Saints  ; 
the  first  and  third  in  H  for  All  Saints  ;  and  the  fourth,  fifth, 
sixth  and  seventh  elsewhere  in  H.  Of  all,  only  the  fourth 
occurs  in  Y,  which  has  special  benedictions  for  All  Saints. 

cantiis  ad  horas  diet  super  ympnos  inxta  cantuni  ymp.   Christe 
redempior. 

In  Y  this  is  the  Hymn  for  first  Vespers  and  Matins.  At 
Lauds  the  hymn  is  lesu  saluator  saeculi,  at  second  Vespers 
Sanctorum  meritis.  So  H,  except  that  at  second  Vespers 
for  the  hymn  is  sung  the  sequence  Ecce  pulcra. 

In  S  at  first  Vespers  and  Matins  the  hymn  is  lesu  saluator, 
and  at  Lauds  and  second  Vespers  Christe  redemptor.  The 
using  of  this  tune  for  all  the  hymns  seems  to  point  to  Christe 
redemptor  being  the  Vespers  hymn  in  the  Gilbertine  use  as  in 
the  York.

f.  58.       I  cannot  find  the  Corpus  Christi  Benedictions  elsewhere, 

p.  106.     Commemoraciones  Sanctorum,  etc. 

This  in  an  index  of  Saints  whose  services  are  to  be  supplied 
from  the  Common. 

The  most  interesting  name  in  it  is  that  of  S.  Hibbald  in 
December.  The  date  here  of  this  somewhat  elusive  saint 
would  seem  to  be  fixed  for  December  14. 

p.  109.    Benedictions.     These  are  quite  different  to  the  collection  given 

in  S,  though  some  of  them  occur. 
In  die  nat.  Domini.     These  are  those  of  feasts  of  nine  lessons  in 

YH.     They  are  not  quite  the  same. 
In  festo   Reliquiarum.     No  soecial  benedictions  are  given  in 

SYH. 

p.  no.    In  festiuitate  omnium  sanctorum.     These  are  not  the  same  as 
those  of  SYH,  which  are  all  different — but  some  are  to  be 
found  in  them. 
Fer.     secunda.    feria     quarta    et    Sabbato.      There    are    none 
corresponding  in  SYH.
